# THE HARD SAYING — Series Bible (SECRET)

> Writers' room only. The player never reads this file. Nothing below may appear in `journal`, `cue`, `key`,
> `previously`, `nextTime`, `logline`, or any player-facing text until the story itself has revealed it on the page.
> Where this bible and `brief.md` conflict, the brief wins. Where this bible and a played episode conflict, the played
> episode wins and this bible gets a revision-log entry.

---

## 0. How to use this document

- **Part I** is the show: premise, spine, style. Every writer reads it before every episode.
- **Part II** is the people. Voices live here. The Voice Editor checks every line against it.
- **Part III** is the hidden architecture: secrets, the central mystery, the arc registry, the season maps, the romance
  roadmap. The Showrunner owns it and adjusts it after every played episode.
- **Part IV** is the world: Marisol, its geography, its rooms, its bars.
- **Part V** is the game, translated into story: what resolve, soul and the relationship axes *look like on the page*.
- **Part VI** is the revision log.

---

# PART I — THE SHOW

## 1. Premise

Fall 2009. Jack Costigan, eighteen, drives two thousand four hundred miles from Youngstown, Ohio, in his laid-off
father's truck to a beach-town public university so good and so godless that his parish priest called it "Babylon with
a surf report." He has come to study philosophy under the famous atheist who once wrote him a one-line postcard, and he
has come carrying a paperback, a confirmation medal, a pack of Marlboro Reds he is quitting, and a secret that a member
of his family is paying for by the day.

On his first night he meets Magdalena Broussard on the back porch of a party neither of them wanted to attend. She is
from New Orleans, she is quicker than he is, she is taken, and she is carrying something too.

Over four years and into the lives after, they will argue about Aquinas and Augustine, about Ted Kennedy and Flannery
O'Connor, about whether you love the world by leaving it or by walking straight into it. They will love other people who
deserve better than to be second. They will be the only two people left standing in a room more than once. And they will
slowly discover that the girl who died at Marisol Point the spring before they arrived is the hinge on which nearly
everyone they love has been quietly turning.

## 2. The thematic spine: the hard saying

John 6. Jesus tells a crowd that has just been fed that he is the bread of life, that unless they eat his flesh and drink
his blood they have no life in them. It is the most scandalous thing he ever says, and he does not soften it. "This is a
hard saying; who can listen to it?" (6:60). "After this many of his disciples drew back and no longer went about with
him" (6:66). Jesus turns to the Twelve: "Do you also wish to go away?" Peter: "Lord, to whom shall we go? You have the
words of eternal life" (6:67–68).

Everything in the show rhymes with that exchange.

- **Love that costs.** Every real love in the show is priced. Danny Costigan pays for Jack's future in years. Frank pays
  for it in a choice he cannot confess. Fr. Osei pays for his vows in silence. Laney pays for Lena's friendship by being
  seen. Julian pays for loving Lena by being asked to stand outside a door he cannot open. Jack and Lena pay for each
  other in waiting.
- **Who stays when others leave.** Wes leaves the Church. Hale left it at seventeen and has been standing outside it in
  the rain ever since. Theo runs. Kevin is told, from two directions, that he must leave something. The show watches who
  stays — at a bedside, in a pew, on a stairwell, on a cliff — and it never pretends staying is easy or that leaving is
  stupid. Peter stays not because he understands but because there is nowhere else to go. That is the show's idea of
  faith: not a feeling, a refusal to leave.
- **The Eucharist as the show's secret center.** Bread that is a body. The ordinary made unbearable. Jack and Lena's
  restraint is, underneath, Eucharistic: the body is not disposable because God hid himself in one. The show never says
  that out loud in narration. Fr. Osei may say it once a season. The rest is in the images: bread, hands, mouths, hunger,
  fasting, the meal after the funeral.
- **Lip service vs. the heart** (the pilot's Gospel, Mark 7): the show's moral epistemology. What comes out of a person
  defiles. Every character performs; the question is what is underneath.

## 3. Tone

*Warmth first, knife second.* Gilmore Girls in the pace and the banter, Hart of Dixie in the sweetness, Greek in the
houses, Task and Strike in the patience of the mystery, Red Dead in the moral weight of one good bad man, The Last of Us
in the way love is the thing that damns and saves. Euphoria only in honesty about bodies and drugs, never in glamour.

Rule of thumb: **three laughs before every cut.** A dark scene earns its darkness by following warmth. A devastating
episode still has a scene where someone eats pie and is ridiculous.

## 4. Style guide

### 4.1 POV and tense
- **Close third, present tense, Jack only.** "Jack watches her cross the porch." The camera never leaves Jack. No
  cutaways, no "meanwhile," no dreams that show what Jack cannot know (a dream may show only what Jack fears).
- Narration knows what Jack knows and sees what Jack sees. It may infer ("something in her face says she has done this
  before"); it may never assert another person's thoughts as fact.
- Jack's interiority is allowed and important, but **dramatized**, not essayed. One sentence of thought, then action.
- The narration's intelligence is Jack's intelligence: a well-read nineteen-year-old from the Rust Belt. It can quote
  Chesterton; it should also notice the price of the gas.

### 4.2 Prose register
- **Literary, warm, funny, sensory. A TV hour on the page.** Scenes are built like television scenes: an establishing
  image (the chyron does the place and time, the first sentence does the light, the weather, the smell), a turn, an exit.
- **Specific nouns.** Not "a drink" — a red Solo cup of Natty Light gone warm. Not "a car" — a 1999 Chevy Silverado with
  rust blooming over the rear wheel wells like a rash. Not "music" — whatever is actually playing.
- **Sensory anchors at Marisol:** eucalyptus (menthol, cat-pee, rain), kelp at low tide, sunscreen, fog that beads on
  wool, the 2 a.m. train horn, sea lions barking off the wharf, jasmine on the Las Olas fences, the sweet chemical smell
  of the Delgado laundry room. **At Youngstown:** wet leaves, the cold metal smell of the garage, Nonna's gravy, Folgers,
  diesel, snow that is gray by noon. **At New Orleans:** sweet olive, boiled crab, rain on hot asphalt, mold, chicory.
- **Sentence music:** vary length. Allow a long, accumulating sentence when Jack is overwhelmed; then a short one.
- **Paragraphs:** dialogue lives inside prose, novel-style. One speaker per paragraph unless it's a rapid-fire exchange
  that reads better stacked.
- **Banned:** the tic registry in `room.md`. Also: "orbs," "padded," "smirked" more than once an episode, "gaze" as a
  verb, "electric" for touch, "a jolt," "time stood still," "the world fell away," "let out a breath he didn't know he
  was holding." No eyes that "darken with desire." No "core."

### 4.3 Dialogue rules
- **Everyone has their own funny.** See voices in Part II. Jack is wry and allusive with Rust Belt bluntness; Lena is
  fast, Southern, combative, theatrical and suddenly tender; Theo is epigrammatic and luxurious; Laney is pageant-Texan
  with filthy timing; Margot is deadpan and polysyllabic; Kevin is dad-joke sunny with a scalpel under it; Osei is
  proverbial and mischievous; Hale is Oxbridge cut-glass with gutter vocabulary.
- **Subtext over text.** People rarely say what they mean when it matters. They talk about the pie. Nobody says "I'm
  scared." They say "Is it cold in here or is it Ohio."
- **2009 mouths.** No therapy-speak ("boundaries," "triggered," "valid," "unpack," "toxic," "lived experience") except
  Margot, who uses academic jargon deliberately and is mocked for it. No "lowkey," "vibe check," "it's giving," "slay,"
  "rizz," "ghosting" (as a verb), "situationship." Era slang is allowed and sparing: "epic fail," "that's what she said,"
  "FML," "sexiled," "hella" (NorCal kids only — Kevin, Julian), "legit," "creeper," "facebook stalking," "the Twilight
  thing," "Team Edward." Jack says "wicked" never; he's from Ohio. Jack says "pop," not "soda." Lena says "y'all," "cher,"
  "making groceries," "neutral ground," "where y'at." Laney says "y'all" too, but Dallas, not New Orleans: "fixin' to."
- **Names:** Lena calls him "Costigan" when fighting, "Jack" when it matters, "Youngstown" when flirting, and once a
  season, "John." Jack calls her "Broussard," "Lena," and, rarely, "Magdalena" — which she allows only from him and only
  when he means it. Theo calls Jack "Costigan," "Father Jack," "my moral compass." People call Theo "Theodore" when he's
  in trouble.
- **Speeches are rare and earned.** One real speech per episode, maximum, usually in the duel or the climax.

### 4.4 How heat is written: restraint is the fire
- The heat is in the **near**: the inch, the almost, the hand that does not move, the breath on a neck in a crowded
  room, the door that stays shut. Proximity, not contact. Specific bodies, specific details: the freckle at the hinge of
  her jaw, the cold of the medal against her collarbone, the way she pushes her sleeves up when she's about to win, the
  stubble he hasn't dealt with, the smell of smoke he can't get out of his jacket.
- **Withholding is active.** When Jack doesn't kiss her, the prose must make the not-kissing an event: show the cost.
- **Desire is good.** Never write desire as dirty. The Catholic claim is that desire is so good it's worth ordering. Jack
  wants her — the prose says so plainly. The restraint is a choice made by a man who wants, not a man who doesn't.
- **When sex is on the page** (other characters, other couples, and later possibly Jack and Lena per player choices):
  frank, literary, adult, specific to the people. Sensation and emotion, the comedy of bodies, the vulnerability, the
  morning after, the loneliness of the wrong bed. Never pornographic mechanics. Never glamorized and never punished by
  the narration; consequences come from character, not from the author's thumb.
- Everyone in sexual content is **18 or older** (see §9 birthdays).
- **HARD RULE (brief): Jack and Lena sleep only with each other, ever.** Neither has slept with anyone before the series,
  and neither sleeps with anyone else during it: not Wes, not Becca, not Sol, not Julian, not Gideon, not a
  stranger at a party. Their first time, whenever and however the player's choices bring it, is with each other. With
  other people the ceiling is **kissing, making out, real physical temptation and the near-miss** — charged, on the page,
  costing resolve — and then the line holds: an interruption, her stop, his stop, a door, a phone, a confession of the
  wrong name. "The loneliness of the wrong bed" belongs to the ensemble, never to Jack or Lena. Jealousy-as-POV may let
  Jack *fear* (a door closing, her coming home late in someone's jacket); the narration must never state or imply as fact
  that Lena slept with anyone, and the fear is answered on the page in S4 ("Never. Not with anyone.").

### 4.5 How faith is written: true, and hard
- **Flannery O'Connor, not a pamphlet.** Grace is real and it is violent, inconvenient, funny, and usually arrives
  through the wrong person. Sin has weight; so does mercy.
- **The sacraments work.** Confession actually absolves. The Eucharist is actually Christ. The narration never winks.
  But the narration never *proves* it either: Jack experiences grace the way people do — as peace he can't account for,
  as a sentence that arrives when he needed it, as the strength to not do the thing.
- **Prayer on the page:** short, real, unpolished. Jack prays like a man talking to someone in the next room. He uses the
  Hail Mary when he can't think, the Jesus Prayer when he's scared, and on bad nights the St. Michael prayer like a
  bouncer's checklist. Lena prays the Memorare and argues with God out loud.
- **Liturgy is accurate:** the real readings for the real Sunday, the real feast days, the real colors. Homilies are
  excerpted, never transcribed whole: two or three sentences that land.
- **Catholics are not the good guys.** Newman has hypocrites, bores, saints, fools, a gossip, a missionary who means it
  and a missionary who's faking it. The faith is true; the faithful are people.
- **The critics get their best arguments.** Margot is often right. Hale is often right. Julian is often the most
  Christ-like person in the room and doesn't believe a word of it. The story never sneers at belief or unbelief.

### 4.6 How the arguments are written
- **Both brilliant, both real.** Real philosophy, real texts, real citations: Aquinas (*ST* I-II q.94 on natural law,
  q.109–114 on grace; *Summa contra Gentiles*), Augustine (*Confessions* VIII, *City of God* XIV, *Enchiridion*,
  "Dilige, et quod vis fac" — *Tractates on 1 John* 7.8), Pascal (*Pensées*, the wager, "cela vous abêtira"), Aristotle
  (*NE* II on habituation), Kierkegaard (*Attack upon "Christendom"*), Newman (*Grammar of Assent*, *Apologia*),
  Anscombe ("Modern Moral Philosophy"), MacIntyre (*After Virtue*), Bernard Williams ("Moral Luck," *Ethics and the
  Limits of Philosophy*), Nietzsche (*Genealogy*), Hume (*Dialogues*), Russell, Mackie (*The Miracle of Theism*), Dennett,
  Hitchens (*god is not Great*, 2007), Simone de Beauvoir (*The Second Sex*), Judith Butler, bell hooks, Mary Daly,
  Dorothy Day, Chesterton (*Orthodoxy*, *The Everlasting Man*), C.S. Lewis, Lewis vs. Tolkien on allegory, O'Connor
  (*Mystery and Manners*, "A Good Man Is Hard to Find," "Revelation," "Parker's Back"), Waugh (*Brideshead*), Greene
  (*The Power and the Glory*, *The End of the Affair*), Percy (*The Moviegoer*, *Love in the Ruins*), Dostoevsky (the
  Grand Inquisitor; Zosima), Weil, Benedict XVI (*Deus Caritas Est*, *Spe Salvi*, *Caritas in Veritate* — July 7, 2009),
  John Paul II (*Theology of the Body*, *Veritatis Splendor*, *Evangelium Vitae*).
- **Quote accurately or paraphrase honestly.** If a writer isn't sure of a quote, paraphrase it in-character ("Pascal says
  something like…").
- **Lena wins about as often as Jack does.** Across a season, tally. Lena's default mode is Thomist-with-a-machete: she
  trusts reason, nature, the body, form, the visible Church; she is merciful toward persons and merciless toward
  arguments. Jack's default mode is Augustinian-with-a-smoker's-cough: he trusts grace, distrusts nature (his own
  especially), feels the fall in his bones, is merciful toward arguments and hard on himself. **They swap positions when
  the facts get personal** — that's where the best duels live.
- **The arguments are flirtation and also real.** Every duel has a moment where it stops being a game. Winning isn't
  always good. Conceding the right point at the right moment is the most romantic thing either of them can do.
- **Politics (reconciled with the brief's map after S1E01):** both are *nearly* politically homeless, both deeply
  pro-life, both convinced that both parties betray the Church, and each thinks the other is wrong for leaning where they
  lean. **Lena** is a Catholic Worker type (Dorothy Day), a pro-life Louisiana Democrat in her bones — union fishermen,
  Charity Hospital, the levees, the death penalty (against), immigrants (for), Katrina as proof the state abandons the
  poor — and ferocious about abortion and euthanasia. **Jack** is more comfortable on the right: a law-and-order Rust Belt
  conservative raised in a Kennedy-Democrat union house (Frank) who broke with it at the kitchen table. Labor is still in
  his blood (his father's layoff is personal) and he distrusts the market's mercy as much as the government's, but his
  instincts run to order, crime and punishment and the culture war, and he votes like it. 🔒 The irony the show never
  states: the law-and-order boy is the reason his brother is in prison. **Over the run (brief):** Lena's struggle deepens
  as the Democrats harden on abortion and as the LGBT movement shifts, in her view, from human dignity to remaking sex and
  the family; Jack keeps hearing racial overtones inside what he considers reasonable disagreements with Obama; the Trump
  years are very hard on Jack (immigration enforcement turning, as he sees it, into dehumanization); Dobbs (2022) is joy
  for both, then ash (mailed pills backed by Republicans too, IVF's expansion); both grow disgusted with the whole system.
  These are convictions written from inside; Margot, Theo, Hale, Kevin, Julian and Yusuf get their best arguments. They
  fight about the health-care bill and Stupak, Notre Dame and Obama (May 2009), Ted Kennedy's funeral, the death penalty,
  immigration, Traficant (Jack: a crook; Frank: *our* crook), and whether the bishops are cowards or courageous about the
  abuse scandal (they agree the bishops failed; they disagree about what that means for obedience).

### 4.7 How comedy works
- **Character comedy, never mockery.** The funniest people are funny on purpose (Lena, Theo, Laney, Margot) and the
  second-funniest are funny by accident (Priya's laminated notes, Joey Abruzzo's volume, Doug Pruitt existing).
- **Running jokes pay off** (see Part IV §26). The scar story. The cigarette reliquary. Margot's whiteboard. The 3 a.m.
  fire alarm. The Anchor's pie ratings.
- **Banter has rhythm**: set, turn, topper — then break the pattern; let someone fail to land one.
- **Jack is funny in a way that keeps people at arm's length.** The show eventually notices that.
- **The sacred is never the butt of the joke**; Catholics are, often.

### 4.8 Content standards (per the brief)
- Sex, desire, bodies, drug use, violence, addiction, overdose, death and grief: frank and literary, HBO-level, always
  in service of character and emotion. Morning-afters, bad sex, good sex, funny sex, the loneliness of a hookup. Never
  pornographic mechanics.
- **Everyone in any sexual content is 18+.** Every freshman in the show is 18 or older at move-in (see §9).
- **Sexual assault:** may be a storyline; if used, it is centered on the survivor's experience and aftermath, never
  titillating, never a twist. **Currently no assault storyline is scheduled.** If one is introduced, it requires a
  Showrunner note and a revision-log entry. **HARD RULE (brief): never in Jack's or Lena's backgrounds, and never
  happening to either of them** — not as a wound, not as the cause of a wound, not as the event behind a secret. Their
  wounds are family rupture, grief, violence, crime, illness, addiction in the family, non-sexual harm, faith crises.
- **HARD RULE (brief): Jack and Lena sleep only with each other** (see §4.4). Applies to backstory and to every season.
- Addiction and overdose: real, unglamorous. Someone may not make it (see §19, Death register).
- **Hard lines:** the narrative never mocks the faith. No cheap villains, no secular strawmen. **Jack and Lena never
  die.**
- Real people (politicians, clergy, journalists, celebrities) appear only through the news, TV, the radio, the paper,
  and characters' opinions about public events. Never invent private conduct for a real person. Never assert, in-world,
  any fact about a real private event that the public record does not support.

---
# PART II — THE PEOPLE

## 5. Dossier format

Each dossier: **Public face** (what Marisol sees) · **Private truth** (what's really going on) · **Wound** · **Want vs.
need** · **Voice** (patterns, humor, habits, sample lines) · **Look** · **Arc**. Items marked 🔒 are hidden canon.

---

## 6. JACK COSTIGAN (the player)

**Full name:** John Anthony Costigan. Confirmation name Michael. Born **March 19, 1991** (St. Joseph's Day) — 18 at
move-in. Youngstown, Ohio (South Side, Brownlee Woods). Cardinal Mooney High School '09: point guard, Latin Club, the kid
who argued with the theology teacher and then served the 6:30 a.m. Mass. Philosophy major. Delgado Hall 412.

**Public face.** The funniest guy on the floor. Charming, quick, a little dangerous, generous with his last twenty,
devastating at 2 a.m. arguments, holds a door, holds a grudge, holds his liquor less well than he thinks. The guy who
goes to Mass hungover and the guy who goes. Smokes when stressed or drinking; is quitting. Reads constantly; the
paperback in his back pocket has margins full of fights with the author.

**Private truth. 🔒** He believes he is not a good man and that everyone who loves him is being conned. He has a reason.
(See §17, Jack's secret.) His charm is a way of paying in advance. His faith is real — not inherited, *chosen*, at a
specific moment in a specific place (a holding cell bench, 🔒 §17) — and it is the one thing he's never performed. He
came to Marisol to fight Adrian Hale because Hale's book nearly took that faith from him at sixteen, and because a part
of him wants to lose.

**Wound.** 🔒 His brother is in prison for something Jack did.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to be worth what it cost. *Need:* to accept that he can't be, and that grace is not a payment
plan.

**Convictions (brief).** Law-and-order Rust Belt conservative (§4.6). **Loves Benedict XVI** — the theologian-pope,
Regensburg, *Spe Salvi*, *Caritas in Veritate*, the liturgy; carries *Spe Salvi* with a Benedict holy card (S1E02); the
resignation (Feb 11, 2013) lands hard. **Struggles with Francis** (says, repeatedly, that Francis has held the line on
doctrine; hates how he treats the conservative cardinals Jack admires). **Adores Leo XIV** (2025). Gets deep into **Bishop
Barron / Word on Fire** from the *Catholicism* series (2011) and later defends him as doing the best he can.

**Voice.** Wry, allusive, Rust Belt blunt. Says "pop," "gumband" (rubber band; Youngstown), "jagoff" (rarely, with
affection), "yinz" never (that's Pittsburgh; he'll correct you). Quotes Chesterton, Springsteen, the Baltimore Catechism,
and his nonna, sometimes in one sentence. Jokes as deflection — when someone gets close, he gets funnier. Under real
pressure, he gets very quiet and very plain. Never says "I'm fine"; says "I'm good," which is worse.
- *"I'm not quitting. I'm tapering. Like a saint, but with Marlboros."*
- *"In Youngstown we don't say 'I love you.' We say 'Text me when you get there,' and then we don't sleep."*
- *"Chesterton says the Church is a house with a hundred gates. I came in through the one with the smoking section."*
- *"I believe in the resurrection of the body. I just don't trust mine."*
- Quiet register: *"Okay. Okay. Tell me what you need."*

**Look (brief).** 6'0", black hair that needs cutting, pale skin, blue eyes, a nose broken once in a CYO basketball
fight (true: St. Christine's vs. St. Brigid's, eighth grade, he started it), stubble by noon. St. Michael medal from his
confirmation. A scar through his left eyebrow whose story he tells differently every time. A battered paperback in his
back pocket. Marlboro Reds. Wardrobe: his brother's old Carhartt jacket (too big in the shoulders — 🔒 Danny's), flannel,
a gray Cardinal Mooney Cardinals hoodie, one good navy blazer from the Goodwill on Market Street for Mass and Hale.
**Phone:** a Motorola RAZR V3 with a cracked hinge, Gina's hand-me-down; T9; he texts like a telegram.

**Romantic history (hard rule).** 🔒 Has kissed girls — a CYO dance at fourteen; Angela Russo from Ursuline for most of
junior year, which ended in the winter after November 2007 when he went "quiet" and she couldn't find the door — and has
never slept with anyone. He is not naive and not scandalized; he is waiting on purpose and it is not easy.

**Arc (full run).** From penance-as-performance to mercy received. S1: the charming rival who can't be honest; Becca, the
tryer — dating him from the beach cleanup on, while Wes is quietly ending, so that when Lena is free, Jack isn't. S2: Becca,
who does everything right, and the sad ending; the knowledge of Theo's secret lands on him. S3: the pull of the world (Sol, from
Theo's LA orbit); holding Theo's secret while asking everyone else for truth. S4: Lena, at last, and the lie that blows it up. S5: exile, Wes's funeral, the seal on
trial. S6: repair. S7–S8: the truth about Danny, told to the people who are owed it; Benedict resigns, Francis arrives;
graduation. Adulthood: he goes home. 🔒 Endgame Jack becomes a high-school theology and philosophy teacher at Cardinal
Mooney, and later a prison chaplaincy volunteer at Trumbull Correctional. He never becomes a priest; the show flirts with
it once (S6) and the flirtation is real.

---

## 7. MAGDALENA "LENA" BROUSSARD

**Full name:** Magdalena Thérèse Broussard. Born **July 22, 1991** (feast of St. Mary Magdalene) — 18 at move-in. New
Orleans, Mid-City (Bayou St. John), the house on N. Dupre Street that took four and a half feet of water in 2005.
Academy of the Sacred Heart ("the Rosary") '09: debate captain, state champion in Lincoln-Douglas, Latin and Greek.
Classics/philosophy. Delgado Hall 217.

**Public face.** Incandescent. The girl who wins the argument, the room, the parking dispute. Funny, fast, theatrical,
generous, impossible to out-talk. Devout without apology: daily Mass when she can manage it, mantilla sometimes, a rosary
in her boot. Loves a party and loves leaving one early. Cooks for the whole floor on Sundays (red beans, a gumbo when she
can get okra). Has a boyfriend back home — everyone on the floor knows by the end of week one, because she says so.

**Private truth. 🔒** She is holding three secrets so tightly she has forgotten what her hands feel like open (§18):
what she did in April 2009 at Marisol; what happened to her grandmother at Memorial Medical Center; and how bad things
have quietly become with Wes. Her fierceness is real and it is also armor. She touches her grandmother's Miraculous Medal
when she's thinking — and when she's lying. She doesn't know she does the second. (The player knows the tell — it's in the
brief — but Jack doesn't, and the player never knows *what* she's lying about. Every touch must be honest bookkeeping.)

**Wound.** 🔒 Katrina, and the question of what her mother did or didn't do on the fourth day. Also: the dead girl whose
bed she slept beside.

**Convictions (brief).** Catholic Worker, Dorothy Day, pro-life Democrat (§4.6). **Respects Benedict XVI's mind and office;
not enthralled** ("I'd follow him into a burning building. I wouldn't put him on my wall"). **Adores Francis** — the
peripheries, mercy, the poor, *Laudato Si'*, the field hospital. **Adores Leo XIV.** Likes Barron quite a bit; grows
dissatisfied with him in the Trump years.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to be right — to have argued every case so well that nothing can be her fault. *Need:* to be
forgiven for something she can't argue her way out of, and to let someone carry it with her.

**Voice.** Fast, Southern, combative, theatrical, then suddenly — without warning — tender and plain. Long sentences that
build to a verdict. Uses "cher" (to Laney and to children), "y'all," "making groceries," "neutral ground," "lagniappe,"
"Lord have mercy" (sincerely and sarcastically). Swears creatively and Catholic-ly: "Sweet suffering Saint Sebastian."
Cites Aquinas like a lawyer cites precedent. Narrates her own outrage in the third person when very angry ("Oh, Magdalena
is *going* to say something"). When hurt she goes formal and polite, which is terrifying.
- *"I'm not against fun. I'm against fun that wants your car keys and your name."*
- *"You're carrying a New Orleans novel to California. That's either a pickup line or a cry for help, and I need to know
  which before I decide how to be disappointed."*
- *"Aquinas says grace perfects nature. He doesn't say it replaces it. You keep trying to skip to the part where God
  does your homework."*
- *"My grandmother's dead and she still makes me wear it."*
- Plain register: *"Don't. Don't be funny right now. Just stay."*

**Look (brief).** Sun-streaked honey-brown waves, golden skin, warm amber-brown eyes; classic Gulf Coast beauty. About
5'5", soft and curvy, at peace with her body, neither hiding nor flaunting it; warmth in every line. Grandmother's worn
gold Miraculous Medal on a thin chain. Thrifted sundresses with beat-up cowboy boots (brown Justins, resoled twice) or
Keds; a cardigan (oatmeal, holes at the elbows) when the fog rolls in. Sometimes veils at Mass in a black lace mantilla.
**Phone:** LG enV2 with the keyboard; texts in complete sentences with punctuation, which Jack finds unbearable and hot.

**Romantic history (hard rule).** 🔒 Wes is her first and only boyfriend (from Feb 2009); before him, a kiss at a Rosary
mixer at sixteen she still rates "a two, generously." She and Wes held the line. She has never slept with anyone.

**Arc (full run).** From advocate to penitent to witness. S1: the rival who won't let anyone see her; Wes unravels; she
loses him — and Jack, who has just said yes to someone else, can only sit on the stairs. S2: the rebound with fire and no
faith (Gideon, her magnetic temptation); Mardi Gras and the Saints; her public debate with Hale; Julian begins late in
the spring. S3: Julian, the tryer who never fights her; Laney's overdose; the Memorial question opened with her mother. S4: Jack — and the confession that costs her
him. S5: Wes's death; the hardest season. S6: forgiveness given and received. S7–S8: the line, the election, the
resignation, the new pope, graduation, a ring. Adulthood: 🔒 Tulane Law, then the Orleans Public Defenders — she argues
for people no one else will. Her mother's secret is finally spoken at Lena's own wedding rehearsal dinner (adult seasons).

---

## 8. THE CORE ENSEMBLE

### 8.1 THEO VANCE
**Full name:** Theodore Ashby Vance. Born **November 3, 1990** — 18 at move-in, 19 in S1E13. Brentwood (father) /
La Cañada Flintridge (mother), Los Angeles. Harvard-Westlake '09 (barely). Undeclared; secretly a film obsessive. Delgado
Hall 412.

**Family.** Father **Richard Vance**, 56, president of production at Meridian Pictures (fictional), a producer credit on a
Best Picture winner (fictional, 2003), on his second marriage (to Brynn, 31, a former development exec). Mother **Caroline
Ashby Vance**, 50, a former actress (one season of a 1980s nighttime soap), Pasadena old money, lapsed Catholic who had
Theo baptized "for Grandmother Ashby," now on her third wellness guru and fourth Pomeranian. Divorced 2004. Sister
**Poppy Vance**, 14, the only person Theo answers the phone for on the first ring.
**Richard's Malibu house (S1E03):** glass and teak on the sand at Broad Beach, west of Zuma, below the PCH; a pool on a
deck over the beach stairs; a screening room nobody uses; a chef-stocked refrigerator with labeled containers nobody is
allowed to eat; the housekeeper (Inés, weekdays) is off for Labor Day. **The Oscar is real** (the producer statuette for the
fictional 2003 Best Picture, *The Long Field*), kept in the downstairs powder room as a joke Richard tells at dinner parties:
so of Theo's pilot "two truths and a lie," the Oscar is true and the lie is rehab or love (🔒 undecided; settle by S2 — the
room leans "never been in love" as the lie). Richard calls Theo **"Teddy"** (only Richard; Theo hates it and never says so).
Richard's rule of six minutes: he stays anywhere six minutes. **(New canon, E04 plan:)** On Sept 11, 2001, Richard was at
the Toronto film festival; with the planes grounded he drove home to Los Angeles with two other producers in a rented
Lincoln — four days — "the longest he's ever stayed anywhere" (Theo). Theo, ten, turned off the kitchen TV so Poppy (six)
wouldn't see; Caroline turned it back on. **(New canon, E05 plan:)** Theo shoots on a **Canon 5D Mark II** (the 2008 video
DSLR every film kid in LA wanted) and keeps it, its lenses and its cables in a **gray camera bag on the top shelf of the
412 closet** — beside the 🔒 black Prada duffel (§20.5). *Point Break* is the one film he loves without irony; when Patrick
Swayze dies (Mon Sept 14, 2009) he watches it at 3 a.m. and says the ending is the kindest thing anyone does in the movie
(🔒 "Utah lets him go"). Poppy has never seen it and is sad because he is. Brynn is kind in a way that costs her nothing and is not a
villain either. **(New canon, E06 plan:)** Theo's cover for the Sunday dawn (🔒 the flowers at the cross) is **"I run now"**
— delivered with too much detail (four miles, barefoot, "very good for the lymphatic system"); he does not run. In a fire
alarm he carries out **both bags off the top shelf, the gray camera bag and the black one, and leaves the espresso machine**
("It's insured") — 🔒 what he saves. Never glossed.

**Public face.** Golden LA prince. Gorgeous, silver-tongued, exhausting, generous to the point of insult. Pays for
everything. Knows every bouncer. Makes everyone feel like the most interesting person at the party for ninety seconds.
Constantly tempting Jack: the drink, the girl, the drive to LA at midnight, the shortcut. Loves Jack immediately and
completely — this is not a performance, and it's the only thing about Theo that isn't.

**Private truth. 🔒** Theo was at Marisol on the night of April 25–26, 2009 (Preview Weekend). He is the one who was on the
cliff when Nora Calloway fell. (§20.) He has not slept more than four hours at a time since. His hedonism is
self-punishment wearing a party hat. He chose Marisol over USC *because* of the Point — he could not be anywhere else. He
leaves fresh flowers at the Point cross every Sunday before dawn. In July 2009, during freshman orientation, he walked into
St. Brendan's Newman Center for the first time in his life and made his first confession since he was eight. Fr. Osei
has known since July what his new parishioner's roommate did.

He latches onto Jack the moment he sees the crucifix go up on the wall. Jack is the nearest thing to grace he can stand
to be near without being looked at by it.

**Wound.** The girl's jacket in his hands. Under that: a father who taught him that every problem has a fixer.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to be punished without being known. *Need:* to be known and survive it.

**Voice.** Epigrammatic, luxurious, cinematic. Speaks in loglines and pitches ("Picture it: a Catholic, a keg, a
cliffside — "). Movie references constantly (Kubrick, Altman, Truffaut, *Point Break* unironically). Calls everyone by
their last name or an invented title ("Costigan," "Father Jack," "Professor," "my moral compass," "Pruitt the Destroyer").
Never says "sorry"; says "I owe you a kidney." When he's lying he gets *more* specific. When he's telling the truth he
gets short.
- *"I'm not a bad influence, Costigan. I'm an influence. The bad is your contribution."*
- *"Pray for me. Preemptively. Like a flu shot."*
- *"My father doesn't have feelings. He has people who have them for him."*
- *"Don't look at me like that. You look like a stained-glass window that's disappointed in me."*
- True register: *"Don't. Okay? Just — not there."*

**Look (brief).** Golden LA prince. Tall, tanned, artfully messy blond hair, green eyes, very expensive clothes worn like
they're cheap (a $400 gray T-shirt with a hole in it, Rag & Bone jeans, Visvim boots, a vintage Rolex Submariner that was
his grandfather's). Looks like a Calvin Klein ad and knows it. **Phone:** iPhone 3GS (June 2009), black, cracked.
**Car:** his grandfather's champagne-gold 1979 Mercedes-Benz 450SL convertible — two seats, the Rolex's twin — parked in
the Anacapa lot on a permit he didn't buy (first seen S1E02). Up all hours with foreign films and subtitles on the MacBook
(*The 400 Blows* at 4 a.m., S1E02); tells people it's "for a class." He is undeclared.

**The alliance (brief).** From S1 Theo runs a covert pro-Jack-and-Lena alliance with Laney. On **Jack's** girls and dates
he is perfectly polite in the open and privately Team Lena, passing Laney information for planning. On **Lena's**
boyfriends after Wes he is openly, inventively rude to every one of them (Gideon: "the Che poster with abs"); Jack mostly
doesn't stop him — until Julian, when Jack tells him to stop because it's turning mean (S3). He **knows Sol from LA**
(Meridian holiday parties since they were twelve) and knows exactly what she is. Never acknowledged aloud until the
reveal (§24.7). **Thanksgiving 2009:** Lena can't go to Youngstown, so Jack invites Theo, and Theo goes (S1E16–E18).

**Arc.** S1: the best friend with a hole in him; the jean jacket in his closet; Evan circling. S2: rushes Sigma Rho,
spirals; the anniversary; in the S2 finale tells Jack the truth on the cliff. S3: tries to get clean; a dangerous romance
with Laney (two self-destroyers); his cocaine is in the room when Laney overdoses. S4: turns himself in on the second
anniversary (April 26, 2011). S5: the legal and family fallout; leaves Marisol; meets Nora's parents in Fresno. S6–S7:
sober, working on a documentary about the closing of an American factory — it's about Lordstown, and Frank Costigan is
in it. S8: returns for graduation; receives Confirmation at the Easter Vigil 2013 with Jack as his sponsor (if the path
supports it; otherwise, he stands in the back). Adulthood: best man. 🔒 Theo lives.

### 8.2 DELANEY "LANEY" PRICE
**Full name:** Delaney Rose Price. Born **February 14, 1991** — 18 at move-in. Highland Park (Dallas), Texas. Highland
Park High School '09. Undeclared ("Business, probably, or marrying it"); 🔒 secretly a math prodigy (800 Math SAT she has
told no one about; wants to be an actuary, which she considers the most shameful possible secret). Delgado Hall 217.

**Family.** Mother **Tammy Price**, 46, ex-Miss Texas runner-up turned pageant coach, tanned to leather, loves Laney like a
project. Father **Gordon Price**, oil-and-gas lawyer, left when Laney was twelve, pays for everything, calls on birthdays
from a different area code each year. Southern Baptist (Park Cities Baptist) — lapsed.

**Public face.** Redhead bombshell, laugh you can hear across a party, former pageant girl (Miss Teen Dallas County 2007
first runner-up), sorority rusher at the top house (Pi Lambda Chi). The wild one. The one who dragged Lena to the party.
Has a Xanax prescription and a doctor's note "for flying" and takes it for everything else. Fiercely loyal: will fight a
man twice her size for a girl she met ten minutes ago.

**Private truth. 🔒** At fifteen she was the passenger in a car driven by her best friend Kaylee Whitmore, who had been
drinking; the car hit a culvert on Preston Road; Kaylee died; Laney walked away with a broken wrist. The Xanax began a
month later. The partying is a way of proving she's alive and making sure she doesn't have to feel it. Lena is the first
person since Kaylee whom Laney has let stay over in her heart. Their friendship is a love story: Laney would die for Lena,
which is the problem.

**Wound.** Kaylee. Being the one who lived.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to be adored and never seen. *Need:* to be seen and stay.

**Voice.** Dallas pageant-Texan, filthy timing, malapropisms she makes on purpose, sugar-coated threats. "Sugar," "y'all,"
"fixin' to," "bless your heart" (weaponized), "I will end you, and I will look fabulous at your funeral." Talks in
superlatives. Narrates her own life like a pageant host ("And our next contestant is a hangover").
- *"Doctor's orders. Literally. I have a note. It's laminated."*
- *"Magdalena Broussard, the Jell-O shots are sentient and they're asking for you."*
- *"You look like a sad Irish poem. Dance with me or I'm calling your mother."*
- *"I'm not a mess, sugar. I'm a *destination*."*
- True register (rare, usually at 4 a.m.): *"Don't leave. I'm not drunk. I mean I am. But don't."*

**Look (brief).** Redhead bombshell. Copper hair, porcelain skin, freckles she covers, a laugh you can hear across a
party. Petite, all hips and attitude. Wardrobe: sundresses that cost more than Jack's tuition, Tory Burch flats, a
monogrammed everything, pearls with a Ramones shirt. **Phone:** BlackBerry Curve, pink.

**The alliance (brief).** Theo's co-conspirator. On **Jack's** girls and dates she is openly rude to every one of them
(Becca, the tryer, from E07, "on principle"); Lena tells her to stop and be nice to all of them *except Sol*, whom Lena
can't stand either — and Laney, rude to Sol in public, privately and begrudgingly likes her (cigarettes on the Pi Lam roof;
never admitted). On **Lena's** boyfriends after Wes she is perfectly polite in the open and privately Team Jack, passing
Theo information. Kevin tells them both to leave it alone; both refuse.

**Arc.** S1: rush, bid day, the Pi Lam world; Xanax creeping; the fight with Lena at midterms. S2: Mardi Gras with the
Broussards — Celeste adopts her on sight; spiral. S3: Theo; the overdose (Nov 2010) — Lena finds her; she lives. S4:
rehab in Arizona over the winter break (Dec 2010 – Jan 2011), back for S4E01, sobriety, the unbearable honesty of the newly sober (comic and painful). S5: actuarial
science, out loud. S6–S8: quietly, impossibly, toward the Church — received into full communion at the Easter Vigil
2013 with Lena as sponsor. Adulthood: maid of honor; an actuary who sponsors other women in AA.

### 8.3 MARGOT LINDQVIST
**Full name:** Margot Ingrid Lindqvist. Born **December 21, 1990** (winter solstice; she considers this a personal brand) —
18 at move-in, 19 in S1E22. Portland, Oregon (Laurelhurst). Lincoln High School '09. Gender and Women's Studies, minor
in Journalism. Delgado Hall 305 (a single; her mother called Residential Life).

**Family.** Mother **Dr. Ingrid Lindqvist**, Reed College, Classics (so Margot can out-Greek Lena when she bothers).
Father **Dr. Paul Hirsch-Lindqvist**, Portland State, Sociology. Open marriage, conducted like a seminar. 🔒 Margot's
mother had a second-trimester abortion in 2000 after a prenatal diagnosis of Down syndrome; the boy would have been
named Anders. Margot, then nine, was told everything in age-appropriate language at the kitchen table. She has defended
her mother's choice in every forum since and has privately grieved Anders every year on the date.

**Public face.** Militant feminist atheist. Clove cigarettes (Djarum Black — the FDA flavored-cigarette ban takes effect
**September 22, 2009** and she holds a wake). Writes for *The Marisol Tide*. Keeps a running score on the Delgado lounge
whiteboard: "USEFUL IDIOTS FOR CHRISTIAN NATIONALISM." Regularly accuses Lena of internalized misogyny, Jack of upholding
the patriarchy, and both of being useful idiots for Christian nationalism. Becomes, for reasons none of them can explain,
one of their closest friends for life.

**Private truth. 🔒** She's lonely in a way her parents' freedom taught her to call independence. She has watched her
mother humiliated in her own kitchen by her father's "honesty." She envies Jack and Lena's certainty and hates that she
does. Anders. She is bisexual and has never said so to anyone, because in her family it would be met with a *reading
list*. She is the best investigator in the show.

**Wound.** Anders. Her parents' freedom.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to win. *Need:* to be accompanied without being argued with.

**Voice.** Deadpan, polysyllabic, merciless, footnoted. Uses academic jargon on purpose and knows how it sounds
("heteronormative," "problematize," "hegemonic," "praxis" — she's allowed 2009 jargon and is mocked for it). Never
raises her voice. Insults as diagnoses. Humor bone-dry and frequently cruel; when she's kind she pretends it's research.
- *"St. Michael. Patron saint of what, bouncers?"*
- *"You're a Catholic at a kegger. Which one of us is the anthropologist?"*
- *"I'm not cold. I'm Scandinavian. It's a different thing."*
- *"You're going to be one of those Catholics who's fun. Those are the dangerous ones."*
- *"Lena, I say this with love: you've internalized a two-thousand-year-old men's club and you call it a personality."*
- True register: *"Can you just — sit here. Don't talk. I'll know if you're praying."*

**Look (brief).** Severe Scandinavian. White-blonde hair cut blunt at the jaw, very pale, thick black-framed glasses, all
black clothing, Doc Martens (oxblood, the only color she owns). Seems to have no body temperature. **Phone:** a Nokia
brick she uses mainly as a paperweight.

**Arc.** S1: the antagonist who keeps showing up; the clove wake (E07); she and Lena's 2 a.m. abortion argument that ends
with both of them crying and neither conceding. S2: starts the Nora anniversary feature for the *Tide*. S3: dates Evan
Marsh (not her TA) and gets closer to the truth than anyone. S4: publishes — responsibly, and it forces the reckoning; she
is right, and it costs Jack and Lena everything for a while. (She likes Julian and tells Jack he's the healthiest thing in
Lena's life — S3, and means it.) S5: the seal story; she and Jack fight about the confessional
like it's a war. She never converts. Adulthood: an investigative reporter (ProPublica-type); godmother to one of Jack
and Lena's children, at her insistence, "as a hedge."
**Anders, as she tells it (E07 plan — the Delgado roof, Wed Sep 23, ~3 a.m., to Lena, Jack and Kevin; 🔒 until then).**
Spring 2000; Margot was nine. Her mother was forty-one; an amniocentesis at about sixteen weeks; trisomy 21. Her parents
decided at the kitchen table and told Margot the same night "in age-appropriate language" (her father's phrase, which she
quotes like a diagnosis). They had already picked the name: Anders. The procedure was on **March 15, 2000** — "the ides; my
father pointed that out; he thought it was interesting." She has defended her mother's choice in every forum since, and
means it; she keeps the date alone every year, and nobody has said his name aloud since 2000 but her. **Never a strawman
in either direction:** she is not converted, softened or "won"; she grieves a brother and defends her mother in the same
breath, and the show holds both. **The same night, after Kevin walks her down, Kevin comes out to her** — the first person he
has ever told (🔒 off-page; Jack sees them leave the roof together and reads it as a romance).

### 8.4 KEVIN TRAN
**Full name:** Kevin Minh Tran (Vietnamese name Trần Minh Khang). Born **February 15, 1991** (Tết, the Year of the
Goat; family lore) — 18 at move-in. San Jose (Evergreen). Bellarmine College Prep '09. Biology, pre-med. Delgado Hall
420, with Doug Pruitt.

**Family.** Father **Trần Văn Hùng** ("Henry"), 58, came by boat in 1979 at age 28 after re-education camp, Pulau Bidong
refugee camp, now owns two nail-salon supply stores. Mother **Nguyễn Thị Lan** ("Lan"), 53, pharmacist at a Walgreens.
Sister **Thủy** ("Tina"), 24, dental school at UOP. Grandmother **Bà Nội** (Trần Thị Mai), 81, prays the rosary in
Vietnamese at 5 a.m. Parish: Our Lady of La Vang, San Jose.

**Public face.** Gym-built pre-med, the nicest guy in any room, Newman stalwart, the one who knows everybody's name and
birthday and remembers your mom's surgery. Altar server, lector, intramural flag football. Dad jokes. Iron-clad public
persona.

**Private truth. 🔒** Kevin is gay and out to exactly one person, Margot (week four of S1 — before that, no one). He came to
Marisol's Preview Weekend in April 2009, met a blond LA kid on the beach stairs behind the Lighthouse, and kissed a boy
for the first time; they had about an hour. That boy was Theo. Around 12:45 a.m. Theo knocked on the door of Kevin's
host's room in Serra Hall, soaked to the knees, his hands scraped raw, holding a woman's denim jacket, and said, "You
never saw me." Kevin let him sleep on the floor. In the morning a girl was dead. Kevin has never asked. He has never told.
He pretends, when they "meet" on Jack's first night, that he's never seen Theo before. Theo pretends too.

**Wound.** The family's sacrifice is a debt he can't repay and can't refuse. Being loved for a person he isn't.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to be good — perfectly, visibly good — so that nothing else can be seen. *Need:* to be loved as
he is by someone who isn't lying either.

**Voice.** Sunny, dad-joke, team-captain. Bay Area "hella" when relaxed (rarely). Organizes things. Medical vocabulary
as comedy ("That's a textbook case of acute Theo"). Under the sunshine a scalpel: the occasional line so precise it
draws blood, followed instantly by a smile. Code-switches to Vietnamese with family; his Vietnamese is worse than he
pretends.
- *"Two truths and a lie: I'm pre-med, I'm an only child, I've never been to a party."*
- *"Respectfully, bro, that's not a hangover, that's a cry for help with a headache."*
- *"I don't drink at these. I'm the designated adult. It's like being designated driver but for your soul."*
- True register: *"You think I don't know what people see? I built what people see. I'm good at it."*

**Look (brief).** Gym-built pre-med. Compact and muscular, a crew cut, an athletic quarter-zip (a collection: navy,
gray, Bellarmine maroon, Stanford-Medicine-summer-program cardinal), a big easy smile that never quite reaches his eyes.
(The brief's phrase; the only sanctioned use.) **Phone:** BlackBerry Bold.

**The alliance.** Kevin is the one who tries to get Theo and Laney to leave Jack and Lena alone ("Respectfully, this is
a hostage situation"); both refuse; he keeps trying, every season, and is in on the joke at the reveal.

**Arc (revised for the player's directive: a long struggle, shaped heavily by his friendship with Jack and Lena, with real
back-and-forth — brief departures from the Church, a same-sex relationship or two, other lapses, a sad attempt with a girl —
before he finally settles as a celibate gay Catholic. Full cost, compassion, no easy answers either way, no mouthpieces.)**
S1: the mask; **out to Margot the night of the clove wake (E07; off-page; Jack misreads them as a couple and teases them
both)**; a stairwell fight with Theo Jack half-hears (E16). S2: Newman VP; pressure from Peter Haddad; Margot as confessor;
**the sad, sincere attempt with a girl** (a Newman sophomore, Clare Dominguez, S2E05–E09 — she deserved better and knew it
within a month; nobody is a villain). S3: comes out to Jack and Lena (they love him, and say the hard true thing, and stay;
Margot is loudly offended on his behalf — at the teaching, and at them). S4: when the Nora case reopens, his night with Theo
becomes evidence — he must decide whether to be a witness and out himself to his family; he does. S5: his father stops
speaking to him; Bà Nội does not. He tries celibacy and the sacraments in earnest, and it doesn't hold yet. S6–S7: a
relationship with a man (Andrés, a med student — kind, serious, not a villain) and a year away from Mass (a semester at the
Episcopal parish in Las Olas). S8: he comes back to sit in the back pew on Holy Thursday 2013. Adulthood: a second
relationship ends; he settles — by his own decision, at full cost — as a celibate gay Catholic; an ER doctor (Osei's
vocation, inherited); godfather to one of Jack and Lena's children. He tells them they are the reason he could imagine it.
**Margot** never accepts the teaching and says so at every table for twenty years, and comes to accept and respect that Jack
and Lena genuinely love him ("I hate that it's real. It's real."). The show accompanies him; it never pretends the cost is
small, and never pretends the life is a defeat.

### 8.5 FR. GABRIEL OSEI
**Full name:** Rev. Gabriel Kofi Osei. Born **May 2, 1964**, Kumasi, Ghana — 45 at the pilot. MB ChB, University of Ghana
Medical School (Korle-Bu), 1989. Internal medicine. Ordained 2003 for the Diocese of Monterey (Marisol falls within it);
chaplain of St. Brendan the Navigator Newman Center since 2006.

**Public face.** Warm, unflappable, laughs from the belly, sees through everyone. A former physician. Fixes the courtyard
sprinkler himself in clerical shirt and rubber boots. Gives short homilies with one sentence you can't stop hearing.
Confessions Saturdays 3:30–4:45 "or whenever you knock — I was a doctor, I am used to being woken." Plays Ghanaian
highlife in his office loudly. Beats students at chess and lets them think it was close. A slight limp he never explains.

**Private truth. 🔒** He was married. **Abena Osei** (née Mensah), a schoolteacher, died on November 14, 1997, on the
Accra–Tema motorway, when Gabriel, driving her to the hospital in labor with their second child, swerved to avoid an
overturned tro-tro. The baby girl (they had named her Esi) died with her. His left leg was crushed; hence the limp. He
was the doctor in the car and could do nothing. Their son **Kwame**, then eight, was sent to Abena's sister in London
(Peckham) while Gabriel recovered — and then Gabriel went to seminary instead of bringing him home. Kwame, now 20, at the
London School of Economics, has not spoken to his father in four years. Gabriel writes him every month.

He holds two sealed confessions about the same night at the Point (🔒 §20): Lena's (April 26, 2009) and Theo's (July
2009). He knows more about Nora Calloway's death than anyone alive except Theo, and he will never say a word of it. Not
to Jack, not to police, not to a judge. The show honors the seal absolutely; it costs him, publicly, in S5.

**Wound.** Abena, Esi, Kwame. The choice of the altar over his son.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to save them all. *Need:* to let God do it, and to be forgiven by his son.

**Voice.** Ghanaian English, unhurried, proverbial, mischievous; drops the occasional Twi word ("Ɛyɛ" — it is well;
"Me da wo ase" — thank you). Physician's metaphors. Asks questions instead of giving answers. Never preaches in
conversation; saves it for the pulpit and keeps it short there.
- *"I spent eight years listening to hearts with a stethoscope and six listening through a screen. The stethoscope lies
  less."*
- *"Youngstown? I know it only from the Springsteen song. Is it as sad as he says?"*
- *"That's not a hockey puck. I was a doctor. That's a ring. Or a tooth."*
- *"The Church is a hospital, my friend. Nobody is embarrassed to be sick in a hospital. Only here."*
- *"When a man jokes that much, it is because the truth is heavy and he is carrying it alone."*

**Look (brief).** Gentle and weathered. Medium height, thin, a graying beard, deep laugh lines, a slight limp he never
explains. Black clerical shirt, sleeves rolled; a wooden rosary on his belt; reading glasses on a cord.

**Osei and Hale, first meeting on the page (E08, Sat Oct 3, the Blessing of the Animals in St. Brendan's courtyard).** Two men
who have heard a great deal about each other — Osei has read *The Consolations of Disbelief* "twice; the second time for the
footnotes"; Hale has heard Osei's homilies "secondhand, from students, which is how the Gospels arrived, I'm told." Hale brings
Bishop Berkeley "to annoy the Ghanaian": **"He's deaf, Father. You'll have to bless him louder."** Osei kneels — slowly; the bad
leg — lays his palm flat on the old dog's ribs and blesses him there, "where he can hear it" (a physician's hands). Then the
fencing: *esse est percipi* — "Then he exists only because Someone is looking at him. You named your dog after the best argument
for God an Irishman ever made." Hale gives the first half of Knox's limerick ("There was a young man who said, God…"); Osei
answers with the reply ("Dear Sir, your astonishment's odd…"). 🔒 When Osei opens the rite with "The Lord be with you," Hale's
lips answer **"And with thy spirit"** — the Prayer Book's response, not the Catholic one of 2009 — and nobody but Jack sees it (one
clause; no gloss; Compline stays S2). Neither man wins. Both enjoy it more than they will admit; this is the start of a long,
wary respect (S2 the Forum; S4 the call; AD the deathbed).

**Arc.** S1: the anchor, quietly watching his two penitents circle the boy he cannot warn. S2: the anniversary Mass at the
Point; Hale's public debate. S3: Kwame comes to Marisol (a conference) and refuses to see him; Jack brokers a dinner. S4:
Theo goes to police; Evan, then the press, realize Osei likely knew. S5: the seal on trial (Ireland's Cloyne Report and
Enda Kenny's speech, July 2011, make the seal front-page news); subpoenaed; refuses to testify about the content of a
confession; held in contempt; five nights in county jail; the Newman community splits. S6: the bishop wants to move him;
the students fight; he stays. Full run: he tells Jack the story of the limp in S6. Adulthood: he witnesses Jack and Lena's
vows. 🔒 Kwame comes to the wedding.

### 8.6 PROF. ADRIAN HALE
**Full name:** Adrian Charles Hale, D.Phil. Born **June 30, 1955**, Barnsley, South Yorkshire — 54 at the pilot. Balliol,
Oxford (PPE, then a D.Phil. on moral luck under Bernard Williams's shadow). Came to Marisol 1998 as the Whitcombe Chair in
Philosophy. Author of *The Consolations of Disbelief* (2006), a bestseller of the New Atheist wave, and three serious
books nobody buys. A regular on cable debates with apologists. Office: Hollis Hall 314, a window on the sea. Lives alone
in a cliffside bungalow in Las Olas with a deaf old greyhound named **Bishop Berkeley** ("Berkeley").

**Public face.** Celebrity public intellectual. Hitchens' swagger, Williams' depth. The lecture course everyone takes
(PHIL 1, "God, Mind and Morals," 400 students) and the honors seminar everyone wants (PHIL 9H, "The Examined Life,"
twelve freshmen, by application). Brilliant, funny, cruel when bored, kind when no one is looking. Drinks too much,
publicly. Loves his best students, maybe too much — not sexually; possessively, paternally, hungrily.

**Private truth. 🔒** Son of an Anglican vicar in a mining town. Served at the altar until seventeen. Lost his faith the
winter his mother died of ovarian cancer, slowly, while his father remained serene; he has never forgiven the serenity.
He still says Compline, drunk, alone, from his father's prayer book, and would rather die than be caught. On his worst nights
he drinks alone in the Anchor's back booth after midnight, orders pie he doesn't eat, recites Housman to the harbor window,
tips "like he's apologizing," and lets Gloria call him a cab (first on-page: Nora's birthday night, S1E04). He collects
believers: for years he has written one-line postcards to devout high-school students who send him rebuttals of his book —
"Come and say it to my face. —A.H." He wrote one to Jack (2008). He wrote one to Lena (2008). He wrote one to Nora
Calloway (2007). On the night of April 25, 2009, at 11:52 p.m., Nora called him from the Point path. He was drunk. The call
lasted forty-one seconds. He said: *"Go home, Nora. You're drunk and you're boring me."* He told the police she'd called
upset about a paper and he'd told her to sleep on it. That's not quite a lie. It's worse.

**Wound.** His mother; his father's peace. Nora's forty-one seconds.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to win one believer from God, to prove that he was right to leave. *Need:* to be found by the
thing he left.

**Voice.** Cut-glass Oxbridge with Yorkshire underneath (the vowels come out when he's drunk or angry: "bath" goes short).
Gutter vocabulary deployed with precision. Epigrams. Calls students by surname. Says "darling" to men and women equally
when drunk. Quotes Larkin, Housman, Hume and the Book of Common Prayer — the last without noticing.
- *"Prove to me you have a soul, Mr. Costigan. You have ninety minutes and I've had lunch."*
- *"Faith, Miss Broussard, is what we call it when a clever girl stops asking questions on purpose."*
- *"I don't hate God. One can't hate a fictional character. One can only hate his fans."*
- *"Come and say it to my face."*
- Drunk, true: *"Lighten our darkness, we beseech thee, O Lord — Christ, listen to me."*

**Look (brief).** Rumpled Oxford rake. Tall, silver hair swept back too long, a linen jacket with wine on the cuff, a
once-beautiful face gone ruddy and broken-veined. Still devastating when he smiles. Reading glasses he loses on his head.

**(E07 plan, `e6_hale_danny`:)** after the friendship duel he gives Jack a pocket *A Shropshire Lad* (a wartime
edition, foxed) "for the brother. No margins. They'd only erase them." 🔒 Kindness when no one is looking, and a leash: the
brother goes into the S3 book.
**(E08 plan:)** Wed Sep 30, St. Jerome — Hale opens with Jerome and the widow Paula ("the scandal of Rome; Mr. Costigan's thesis
in a hair shirt"); the week's subject is **lying** (Augustine, *De mendacio*; Kant's murderer at the door; Aquinas II-II q.110).
Jack and Lena, scrupulously friends, agree with each other all hour; Hale is appalled ("Somebody fetch a priest; the Catholics are
agreeing"). 🔒 His one unguarded line: **"The commonest lie in the world is not told to murderers at doors. It's told to
policemen, about the dead, by people who loved them."** (His own forty-one seconds; Lena's statement; the Costigans' plea — he knows
only the first.) He looks at the window after it. Sat Oct 3: the Blessing (see Osei, §8.5).
**Arc.** S1: the seduction of two minds; the postcard discovery (E14); the dinner party where he's magnificent and then
vile. S2: debates Lena publicly at the Marisol Forum and loses; loves her more for it. S3: writes a book on moral luck
using his students as unnamed examples; Jack finds himself in a footnote. S4: the forty-one seconds come out; disgrace;
leave of absence. S5: sobriety, attempted. S6: cirrhosis. S8: his last lecture is the Philosophy commencement address.
Adulthood: 🔒 dies in 2015; asks for Fr. Osei; what happens in the room is left ambiguous. The show never claims a
deathbed conversion. It lets Jack see Hale's hand move toward his forehead, and stop, or not.

### 8.7 JULIAN PARK-ELLIS
**Full name:** Julian Min-jun Park-Ellis. Born **January 9, 1990** — 19 at the pilot. Oakland (Rockridge). Oakland School
for the Arts '08. Sophomore, Music Composition. Lives off campus in Las Olas (a converted garage behind a surf house on
Del Playa Lane, with a piano he got for free and tuned himself).

**Family.** Mother **Park Soo-jin** ("Susan"), cellist in the Oakland East Bay Symphony. Father **Marcus Ellis**, high-school
chemistry teacher and weekend jazz bassist. Both secular, loving, a little worried about him. An older brother, **Daniel**,
in the Navy.

**Public face.** Quietly handsome; calm; the one you'd trust with anything. Plays guitar and piano, sings at Café Bastille's
Thursday open mic, records songs in his garage onto a Tascam four-track. Secular; gentle; heartbreakingly decent.
Everyone's favorite person to sit next to.

**Private truth. 🔒** He was Nora Calloway's closest friend and her duet partner; they sang at the Point wall at night
freshman year. He loved her; she loved someone she wouldn't name. He wrote a song after she died — "Fresno" — that he plays
once a month and can never finish without his voice going. He doesn't know about the pregnancy or Evan. He suspects
suicide and blames himself for not seeing it.

**Wound.** Nora. Being the friend.

**Want vs. need.** *Want:* to be chosen first by someone. *Need:* to stop accepting second place as proof of goodness.

**Voice.** Soft-spoken, wry, precise; musical metaphors ("that's a suspended chord, it wants to resolve"). Asks real
questions and waits for the answer. Rarely jokes; when he does, it's sly and kind. Says "hella" once a season.
- *"I don't believe any of it. But I believe you do, and I'd like to know what that's like. Is that allowed?"*
- *"Every song I like is in a key that sounds like church. I'm told that's not an accident."*
- *"You don't have to convert me. You just have to let me stand next to you while you do the thing."*

**Look (brief).** Quietly handsome. Medium height, clean-cut, warm brown skin, short dark hair, a calm presence; you'd trust
him with anything. Musician. Wears his father's old cardigans, a Timex, guitar calluses.

**The fatal gap (brief: both).** He respects her faith without ever believing it — he stands lovingly outside the church
door, and no sacramental marriage is possible — *and* he's too easy: gentle and deferential, he never fights her, and she
discovers she needs to be argued with. Perfect on paper and wrong in the bones; the ending is sad. **Hard rule:** they
never sleep together; he never asks her to (it's one of the things that makes him heartbreaking). Theo is openly rude to
him until Jack, who can tell it won't work out, tells Theo to stop (S3E12); Laney is polite and feeds Theo; Margot thinks
he's the healthiest thing in Lena's life and says so.

**Arc.** S1: glimpsed playing on the party stairs; Lena listens; "Fresno" at the open mic (E04); a friend. S2: Lena and
Julian, from late March (S2E15, after Gideon). He reads *Confessions* for her; comes to Easter Vigil and stands at the
back. S3: the tryer: RCIA inquiry sessions (he goes; he never asks to be received), Thanksgiving in New Orleans with the
Broussards, the honest ending in December ("I'd lie for you if you'd let me. You won't let me." — and hers, the true
one: "You've never once told me I was wrong."). S4: learns what Lena hid about Nora; the hardest scene he has. Later: plays at the
wedding. Never converts; never stops being decent.
